Yeah… I do. I eat fast. Faster than makes any sense, really.

It’s like I’m back on some tight delivery schedule, you know, trying to make time between stops—but I’m not in a truck, I’m just sitting there with food. I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.

It’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.

I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.

Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.

I mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.

It’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.

But actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.

Several nights, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty while you're eating all that food, or is the guilt something that comes afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s more afterward, I think. While I’m doing it, I’m kind of… caught up in it. Like my head goes quiet in the wrong way.\n\nBut after, that’s when it hits me. I’ll be sitting there looking at all the empty containers, wrappers, bowls—whatever it is—and I just feel disgusted with myself. Like, what happened to me? I’ve always tried to be a steady man, in control, the kind of guy people could count on. And then there I am, staring at the mess, feeling like I don’t even recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast. Faster than makes any sense, really.\n\nIt’s like I’m back on some tight delivery schedule, you know, trying to make time between stops—but I’m not in a truck, I’m just sitting there with food. I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is how I feel after I do it. I’ll be sitting there, you know, looking at all the empty containers, and I just feel… disgusted with myself. Like I’m not the steady, in-control kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI mean, I’ve driven trucks, worked sales routes, kept things moving for years—you get used to being the guy who handles things. But then afterward, sitting there with the mess in front of me, it’s like… I don’t recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you do that leaves you with all those empty containers?\n\nPatient: It’s the eating. Like… some evenings I’ll tell myself I’m just going to heat up one bowl of pozole, nothing big, just dinner. And then somehow I’m back in the kitchen again, and then again, and before I really face what I’m doing, the pot’s gone.\n\nAnd it’s not just that. I’ll have a couple tamales, then chips, then whatever leftovers are sitting there. It’s like I’m feeding a whole work crew, you know? I used to be around guys on routes who could put away food after a long day, and I’ll think about that, but… it’s just me. Just me sitting there with all of it gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t. Once I start, it’s like the discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off. I mean, driving all those miles, keeping schedules, watching fuel, paperwork—you had to stay on top of yourself.\n\nBut with the food, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. And then my hand just goes back for more anyway. It’s embarrassing to say, but in that moment I don’t feel like I’m the one steering anymore.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks, I’d say. It kind of blends together, honestly. When I was working routes, at least the days had a shape to them, you know—stops, miles, checking in, all that.\n\nBut these last three months being home, I can’t really think of a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks it’s more than that. Several nights, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty while you're eating all that food, or is the guilt something that comes afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s more afterward, I think. While I’m doing it, I’m kind of… caught up in it. Like my head goes quiet in the wrong way.\n\nBut after, that’s when it hits me. I’ll be sitting there looking at all the empty containers, wrappers, bowls—whatever it is—and I just feel disgusted with myself. Like, what happened to me? I’ve always tried to be a steady man, in control, the kind of guy people could count on. And then there I am, staring at the mess, feeling like I don’t even recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast. Faster than makes any sense, really.\n\nIt’s like I’m back on some tight delivery schedule, you know, trying to make time between stops—but I’m not in a truck, I’m just sitting there with food. I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.

A lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.

So… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.

Several nights, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty while you're eating all that food, or is the guilt something that comes afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s more afterward, I think. While I’m doing it, I’m kind of… caught up in it. Like my head goes quiet in the wrong way.\n\nBut after, that’s when it hits me. I’ll be sitting there looking at all the empty containers, wrappers, bowls—whatever it is—and I just feel disgusted with myself. Like, what happened to me? I’ve always tried to be a steady man, in control, the kind of guy people could count on. And then there I am, staring at the mess, feeling like I don’t even recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast. Faster than makes any sense, really.\n\nIt’s like I’m back on some tight delivery schedule, you know, trying to make time between stops—but I’m not in a truck, I’m just sitting there with food. I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Faster than makes any sense, really.\n\nIt’s like I’m back on some tight delivery schedule, you know, trying to make time between stops—but I’m not in a truck, I’m just sitting there with food. I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. Once I start, it’s like the same discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off.\n\nI mean, when I was driving, I could stick to a schedule, watch my hours, do what needed doing even when I didn’t feel like it. But with this, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down,” and my hand still goes back for more. Like I’m hearing myself, but it doesn’t seem to stop anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m not sure what counts as “tried,” exactly.

I mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I told myself I ought to do better, you know, like you do with anything. Back when I was working routes, if I got behind or started grabbing food from gas stations, I’d say, “Alright, tomorrow I’m going to be more sensible,” but then tomorrow comes and you’re thinking about deliveries, traffic, where the next stop is, all that.

I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. Once I start, it’s like the same discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off.\n\nI mean, when I was driving, I could stick to a schedule, watch my hours, do what needed doing even when I didn’t feel like it. But with this, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down,” and my hand still goes back for more. Like I’m hearing myself, but it doesn’t seem to stop anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to cut down on this kind of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as “tried,” exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I told myself I ought to do better, you know, like you do with anything. Back when I was working routes, if I got behind or started grabbing food from gas stations, I’d say, “Alright, tomorrow I’m going to be more sensible,” but then tomorrow comes and you’re thinking about deliveries, traffic, where the next stop is, all that.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve made a real plan to cut down, if that’s what you mean. I’ve thought about it, sure, but I’m not sure I’d call it a proper attempt.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t know that I’ve really avoided things like that, no. Not in any organized way.

I mean, there are foods I’ll think, “Well, maybe don’t bring that home,” but then I’m at the store and I’m thinking about whether the truck needs gas, or whether I forgot something, and next thing I know I’m comparing prices on crackers or whatever. Funny what sticks in your head.

When I was working routes, at least the days had a shape to them, you know—stops, miles, checking in, all that.\n\nBut these last three months being home, I can’t really think of a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks it’s more than that. Several nights, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty while you're eating all that food, or is the guilt something that comes afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s more afterward, I think. While I’m doing it, I’m kind of… caught up in it. Like my head goes quiet in the wrong way.\n\nBut after, that’s when it hits me. I’ll be sitting there looking at all the empty containers, wrappers, bowls—whatever it is—and I just feel disgusted with myself. Like, what happened to me? I’ve always tried to be a steady man, in control, the kind of guy people could count on. And then there I am, staring at the mess, feeling like I don’t even recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast. Faster than makes any sense, really.\n\nIt’s like I’m back on some tight delivery schedule, you know, trying to make time between stops—but I’m not in a truck, I’m just sitting there with food. I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. Once I start, it’s like the same discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off.\n\nI mean, when I was driving, I could stick to a schedule, watch my hours, do what needed doing even when I didn’t feel like it. But with this, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down,” and my hand still goes back for more. Like I’m hearing myself, but it doesn’t seem to stop anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to cut down on this kind of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as “tried,” exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I told myself I ought to do better, you know, like you do with anything. Back when I was working routes, if I got behind or started grabbing food from gas stations, I’d say, “Alright, tomorrow I’m going to be more sensible,” but then tomorrow comes and you’re thinking about deliveries, traffic, where the next stop is, all that.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve made a real plan to cut down, if that’s what you mean. I’ve thought about it, sure, but I’m not sure I’d call it a proper attempt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to avoid certain foods or situations because you thought they might trigger one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really avoided things like that, no. Not in any organized way.\n\nI mean, there are foods I’ll think, “Well, maybe don’t bring that home,” but then I’m at the store and I’m thinking about whether the truck needs gas, or whether I forgot something, and next thing I know I’m comparing prices on crackers or whatever. I used to know every good place to stop on certain routes—some stations had better coffee, one had those sandwiches that looked terrible but somehow you’d still get one. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut as far as actually saying, “I can’t go there” or “I have to stay away from that food because it’ll set me off”… I’m not sure I’ve done that. Not clearly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine if the patient has made any attempts to compensate for the eaten calories", "question": "Have you ever done anything to try to make up for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes?"}

Most weeks it’s more than that. Several nights, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty while you're eating all that food, or is the guilt something that comes afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s more afterward, I think. While I’m doing it, I’m kind of… caught up in it. Like my head goes quiet in the wrong way.\n\nBut after, that’s when it hits me. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. Once I start, it’s like the same discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off.\n\nI mean, when I was driving, I could stick to a schedule, watch my hours, do what needed doing even when I didn’t feel like it. Like I’m hearing myself, but it doesn’t seem to stop anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to cut down on this kind of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as “tried,” exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I told myself I ought to do better, you know, like you do with anything. Back when I was working routes, if I got behind or started grabbing food from gas stations, I’d say, “Alright, tomorrow I’m going to be more sensible,” but then tomorrow comes and you’re thinking about deliveries, traffic, where the next stop is, all that.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve made a real plan to cut down, if that’s what you mean. I’ve thought about it, sure, but I’m not sure I’d call it a proper attempt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to avoid certain foods or situations because you thought they might trigger one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really avoided things like that, no. Not in any organized way.\n\nI mean, there are foods I’ll think, “Well, maybe don’t bring that home,” but then I’m at the store and I’m thinking about whether the truck needs gas, or whether I forgot something, and next thing I know I’m comparing prices on crackers or whatever. Not clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know what you mean by “make up for it,” exactly.\n\nLike, I’ve told myself, “Well, you shouldn’t have done that,” and maybe the next day I’ll think I ought to be more careful. No, not like vomiting or going out to exercise because of it. I mean, I might have thought, “I should take a walk more,” but that’s the kind of thing I say about everything now that I’m older.

Back when I was driving, I got plenty of walking in some days just from loading and unloading, depending on the stop. Mostly I’d just feel bad about it and tell myself I shouldn’t do that again.

I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. Once I start, it’s like the same discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off.\n\nI mean, when I was driving, I could stick to a schedule, watch my hours, do what needed doing even when I didn’t feel like it. But with this, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down,” and my hand still goes back for more. Like I’m hearing myself, but it doesn’t seem to stop anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to cut down on this kind of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as “tried,” exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I told myself I ought to do better, you know, like you do with anything. Back when I was working routes, if I got behind or started grabbing food from gas stations, I’d say, “Alright, tomorrow I’m going to be more sensible,” but then tomorrow comes and you’re thinking about deliveries, traffic, where the next stop is, all that.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve made a real plan to cut down, if that’s what you mean. I’ve thought about it, sure, but I’m not sure I’d call it a proper attempt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to avoid certain foods or situations because you thought they might trigger one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really avoided things like that, no. Not in any organized way.\n\nI mean, there are foods I’ll think, “Well, maybe don’t bring that home,” but then I’m at the store and I’m thinking about whether the truck needs gas, or whether I forgot something, and next thing I know I’m comparing prices on crackers or whatever. I used to know every good place to stop on certain routes—some stations had better coffee, one had those sandwiches that looked terrible but somehow you’d still get one. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut as far as actually saying, “I can’t go there” or “I have to stay away from that food because it’ll set me off”… I’m not sure I’ve done that. Not clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know what you mean by “make up for it,” exactly.\n\nLike, I’ve told myself, “Well, you shouldn’t have done that,” and maybe the next day I’ll think I ought to be more careful. But I don’t know that I’ve done some specific thing because of it.\n\nIt’s funny, when I was driving, if I made up time on a route, that was real clear — you left early, skipped a long stop, knew where the traffic backed up. With food, it’s not as neat as that. Mostly I just sit there afterward thinking I overdid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to do something to compensate for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, not like vomiting or going out to exercise because of it. I mean, I might have thought, “I should take a walk more,” but that’s the kind of thing I say about everything now that I’m older.\n\nBack when I was driving, I got plenty of walking in some days just from loading and unloading, depending on the stop. Other days you’d sit so long your back would get stiff, and then you’d say you needed to move around more. But it wasn’t like I ate too much and then had a plan to burn it off or get rid of it.\n\nMostly I’d just feel bad about it and tell myself I shouldn’t do that again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t think so. I mean, not as a thing after eating, no.

I’ve taken stuff before if I was, you know, backed up or whatever, but not like, “I ate too much, now I need to take this.” And diuretics… those water pills, right? I don’t think I’ve used anything like that for food.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is how I feel after I do it. I’ll be sitting there, you know, looking at all the empty containers, and I just feel… disgusted with myself. Like I’m not the steady, in-control kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI mean, I’ve driven trucks, worked sales routes, kept things moving for years—you get used to being the guy who handles things. But then afterward, sitting there with the mess in front of me, it’s like… I don’t recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you do that leaves you with all those empty containers?\n\nPatient: It’s the eating. Like… some evenings I’ll tell myself I’m just going to heat up one bowl of pozole, nothing big, just dinner. And then somehow I’m back in the kitchen again, and then again, and before I really face what I’m doing, the pot’s gone.\n\nAnd it’s not just that. I’ll have a couple tamales, then chips, then whatever leftovers are sitting there. It’s like I’m feeding a whole work crew, you know? I used to be around guys on routes who could put away food after a long day, and I’ll think about that, but… it’s just me. Just me sitting there with all of it gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t. Once I start, it’s like the discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off. I mean, driving all those miles, keeping schedules, watching fuel, paperwork—you had to stay on top of yourself.\n\nBut with the food, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. And then my hand just goes back for more anyway. It’s embarrassing to say, but in that moment I don’t feel like I’m the one steering anymore.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks, I’d say. It kind of blends together, honestly. When I was working routes, at least the days had a shape to them, you know—stops, miles, checking in, all that.\n\nBut these last three months being home, I can’t really think of a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks it’s more than that. Several nights, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty while you're eating all that food, or is the guilt something that comes afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s more afterward, I think. While I’m doing it, I’m kind of… caught up in it. Like my head goes quiet in the wrong way.\n\nBut after, that’s when it hits me. I’ll be sitting there looking at all the empty containers, wrappers, bowls—whatever it is—and I just feel disgusted with myself. Like, what happened to me? I’ve always tried to be a steady man, in control, the kind of guy people could count on. And then there I am, staring at the mess, feeling like I don’t even recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast. Faster than makes any sense, really.\n\nIt’s like I’m back on some tight delivery schedule, you know, trying to make time between stops—but I’m not in a truck, I’m just sitting there with food. I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. Once I start, it’s like the same discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off.\n\nI mean, when I was driving, I could stick to a schedule, watch my hours, do what needed doing even when I didn’t feel like it. But with this, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down,” and my hand still goes back for more. Like I’m hearing myself, but it doesn’t seem to stop anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to cut down on this kind of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as “tried,” exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I told myself I ought to do better, you know, like you do with anything. Back when I was working routes, if I got behind or started grabbing food from gas stations, I’d say, “Alright, tomorrow I’m going to be more sensible,” but then tomorrow comes and you’re thinking about deliveries, traffic, where the next stop is, all that.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve made a real plan to cut down, if that’s what you mean. I’ve thought about it, sure, but I’m not sure I’d call it a proper attempt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to avoid certain foods or situations because you thought they might trigger one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really avoided things like that, no. Not in any organized way.\n\nI mean, there are foods I’ll think, “Well, maybe don’t bring that home,” but then I’m at the store and I’m thinking about whether the truck needs gas, or whether I forgot something, and next thing I know I’m comparing prices on crackers or whatever. I used to know every good place to stop on certain routes—some stations had better coffee, one had those sandwiches that looked terrible but somehow you’d still get one. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut as far as actually saying, “I can’t go there” or “I have to stay away from that food because it’ll set me off”… I’m not sure I’ve done that. Not clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know what you mean by “make up for it,” exactly.\n\nLike, I’ve told myself, “Well, you shouldn’t have done that,” and maybe the next day I’ll think I ought to be more careful. But I don’t know that I’ve done some specific thing because of it.\n\nIt’s funny, when I was driving, if I made up time on a route, that was real clear — you left early, skipped a long stop, knew where the traffic backed up. With food, it’s not as neat as that. Mostly I just sit there afterward thinking I overdid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to do something to compensate for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, not like vomiting or going out to exercise because of it. I mean, I might have thought, “I should take a walk more,” but that’s the kind of thing I say about everything now that I’m older.\n\nBack when I was driving, I got plenty of walking in some days just from loading and unloading, depending on the stop. Other days you’d sit so long your back would get stiff, and then you’d say you needed to move around more. But it wasn’t like I ate too much and then had a plan to burn it off or get rid of it.\n\nMostly I’d just feel bad about it and tell myself I shouldn’t do that again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not as a thing after eating, no.\n\nI’ve taken stuff before if I was, you know, backed up or whatever, but not like, “I ate too much, now I need to take this.” And diuretics… those water pills, right? I don’t think I’ve used anything like that for food.\n\nIt reminds me, my dad used to keep all kinds of old bottles in the medicine cabinet, half of them you couldn’t even read the labels anymore. I always thought that was a bad idea, but then you get older and suddenly your own bathroom cabinet starts looking the same way. But no, not for those episodes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know what you mean by “make up for it,” exactly.\n\nLike, I’ve told myself, “Well, you shouldn’t have done that,” and maybe the next day I’ll think I ought to be more careful. But I don’t know that I’ve done some specific thing because of it.\n\nIt’s funny, when I was driving, if I made up time on a route, that was real clear — you left early, skipped a long stop, knew where the traffic backed up. With food, it’s not as neat as that. Mostly I just sit there afterward thinking I overdid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to do something to compensate for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, not like vomiting or going out to exercise because of it. I mean, I might have thought, “I should take a walk more,” but that’s the kind of thing I say about everything now that I’m older.\n\nBack when I was driving, I got plenty of walking in some days just from loading and unloading, depending on the stop. Other days you’d sit so long your back would get stiff, and then you’d say you needed to move around more. But it wasn’t like I ate too much and then had a plan to burn it off or get rid of it.\n\nMostly I’d just feel bad about it and tell myself I shouldn’t do that again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m not really sure. I mean, I know my weight’s gone up and down over the years, but I couldn’t tell you if it’s been a significant amount since these episodes started.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is how I feel after I do it. I’ll be sitting there, you know, looking at all the empty containers, and I just feel… disgusted with myself. Like I’m not the steady, in-control kind of man I’ve always tried to be.\n\nI mean, I’ve driven trucks, worked sales routes, kept things moving for years—you get used to being the guy who handles things. But then afterward, sitting there with the mess in front of me, it’s like… I don’t recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you do that leaves you with all those empty containers?\n\nPatient: It’s the eating. Like… some evenings I’ll tell myself I’m just going to heat up one bowl of pozole, nothing big, just dinner. And then somehow I’m back in the kitchen again, and then again, and before I really face what I’m doing, the pot’s gone.\n\nAnd it’s not just that. I’ll have a couple tamales, then chips, then whatever leftovers are sitting there. It’s like I’m feeding a whole work crew, you know? I used to be around guys on routes who could put away food after a long day, and I’ll think about that, but… it’s just me. Just me sitting there with all of it gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: No… no, I don’t. Once I start, it’s like the discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off. I mean, driving all those miles, keeping schedules, watching fuel, paperwork—you had to stay on top of yourself.\n\nBut with the food, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, Magin, put the fork down. That’s enough. And then my hand just goes back for more anyway. It’s embarrassing to say, but in that moment I don’t feel like I’m the one steering anymore.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: Since I’ve been home with this back injury, it’s been… several nights most weeks, I’d say. It kind of blends together, honestly. When I was working routes, at least the days had a shape to them, you know—stops, miles, checking in, all that.\n\nBut these last three months being home, I can’t really think of a week where it didn’t happen at least once. Most weeks it’s more than that. Several nights, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty while you're eating all that food, or is the guilt something that comes afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s more afterward, I think. While I’m doing it, I’m kind of… caught up in it. Like my head goes quiet in the wrong way.\n\nBut after, that’s when it hits me. I’ll be sitting there looking at all the empty containers, wrappers, bowls—whatever it is—and I just feel disgusted with myself. Like, what happened to me? I’ve always tried to be a steady man, in control, the kind of guy people could count on. And then there I am, staring at the mess, feeling like I don’t even recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I eat fast. Faster than makes any sense, really.\n\nIt’s like I’m back on some tight delivery schedule, you know, trying to make time between stops—but I’m not in a truck, I’m just sitting there with food. I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. Once I start, it’s like the same discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off.\n\nI mean, when I was driving, I could stick to a schedule, watch my hours, do what needed doing even when I didn’t feel like it. But with this, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down,” and my hand still goes back for more. Like I’m hearing myself, but it doesn’t seem to stop anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to cut down on this kind of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as “tried,” exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I told myself I ought to do better, you know, like you do with anything. Back when I was working routes, if I got behind or started grabbing food from gas stations, I’d say, “Alright, tomorrow I’m going to be more sensible,” but then tomorrow comes and you’re thinking about deliveries, traffic, where the next stop is, all that.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve made a real plan to cut down, if that’s what you mean. I’ve thought about it, sure, but I’m not sure I’d call it a proper attempt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to avoid certain foods or situations because you thought they might trigger one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really avoided things like that, no. Not in any organized way.\n\nI mean, there are foods I’ll think, “Well, maybe don’t bring that home,” but then I’m at the store and I’m thinking about whether the truck needs gas, or whether I forgot something, and next thing I know I’m comparing prices on crackers or whatever. I used to know every good place to stop on certain routes—some stations had better coffee, one had those sandwiches that looked terrible but somehow you’d still get one. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut as far as actually saying, “I can’t go there” or “I have to stay away from that food because it’ll set me off”… I’m not sure I’ve done that. Not clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know what you mean by “make up for it,” exactly.\n\nLike, I’ve told myself, “Well, you shouldn’t have done that,” and maybe the next day I’ll think I ought to be more careful. But I don’t know that I’ve done some specific thing because of it.\n\nIt’s funny, when I was driving, if I made up time on a route, that was real clear — you left early, skipped a long stop, knew where the traffic backed up. With food, it’s not as neat as that. Mostly I just sit there afterward thinking I overdid it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to do something to compensate for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes, like exercising or vomiting?\n\nPatient: No, not like vomiting or going out to exercise because of it. I mean, I might have thought, “I should take a walk more,” but that’s the kind of thing I say about everything now that I’m older.\n\nBack when I was driving, I got plenty of walking in some days just from loading and unloading, depending on the stop. But it wasn’t like I ate too much and then had a plan to burn it off or get rid of it.\n\nMostly I’d just feel bad about it and tell myself I shouldn’t do that again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is how I feel after I do it. I’ll be sitting there, you know, looking at all the empty containers, and I just feel… disgusted with myself. But then afterward, sitting there with the mess in front of me, it’s like… I don’t recognize myself.\n\nDoctor: What is it that you do that leaves you with all those empty containers?\n\nPatient: It’s the eating. Like… some evenings I’ll tell myself I’m just going to heat up one bowl of pozole, nothing big, just dinner. Once I start, it’s like the discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off. Most weeks it’s more than that. Several nights, like I said.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty while you're eating all that food, or is the guilt something that comes afterward?\n\nPatient: It’s more afterward, I think. While I’m doing it, I’m kind of… caught up in it. Like my head goes quiet in the wrong way.\n\nBut after, that’s when it hits me. I’ll barely even taste it sometimes. Just shoveling it in, one bite after another, and then I catch myself breathing hard because I haven’t slowed down enough to even… I don’t know, chew like a normal person.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. I used to be real careful with timing on the road, but with this, it’s like that same hurry gets twisted into something else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure or enjoyment while you're eating all that food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it pleasure, exactly. I mean… maybe for a minute there’s something, like it fills up the space, but I’m not sitting there enjoying it like a good meal.\n\nIt’s not like when I used to stop at this little diner off Route 6—place had real good pie, and you’d actually taste it, talk to the waitress, feel like a person. This is different. I’m eating, but I’m not really… enjoying. Or I don’t notice it much, anyway. It’s more like I’m just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any relief after these episodes of eating large amounts of food are over?\n\nPatient: Relief? I’m not sure I’d put it that way.\n\nI mean, sometimes there’s a kind of quiet after, like when you finally pull the truck into a lot after a long stretch and everything stops humming for a second. But I don’t know if that’s relief or just… being done.\n\nIt’s hard to explain. I’ll be sitting there, and it’s over, and part of me is just tired. Then my mind starts wandering—like I’ll think about needing to take the trash out, or how I used to keep the cab real clean when I was driving, because a messy cab made the whole day feel wrong. Funny what sticks with you.\n\nBut actual relief? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat like this when you're feeling stressed or anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure. I mean, I know stress is around a lot, sure, but whether that’s what sets it off every time… I haven’t kept track like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I’m just going to get something quick, and then it turns into more than that, but I couldn’t always tell you what I was feeling right before. Back when I was driving, stress was easy to name—bad traffic, late delivery, dispatcher calling. You knew what it was. Now it’s more muddled.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes? But I don’t want to say yes if I haven’t really noticed it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by feeling upset or emotional?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Upset or emotional… I mean, that could be in there sometimes, but I don’t catch it clean enough to say that’s usually what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time I only notice once I’m already eating, and then afterward I try to think back, like, “What was I feeling?” but it’s kind of a blur. Reminds me of missing a turn on a delivery route—by the time you realize it, you’re already a few miles down the road and trying to figure out where it happened.\n\nSo… maybe, but I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a usual trigger like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. Once I start, it’s like the same discipline I used to have on the road just shuts off.\n\nI mean, when I was driving, I could stick to a schedule, watch my hours, do what needed doing even when I didn’t feel like it. But with this, I’ll be sitting there telling myself, “Magin, put the fork down,” and my hand still goes back for more. Like I’m hearing myself, but it doesn’t seem to stop anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to cut down on this kind of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure what counts as “tried,” exactly.\n\nI mean, I’ve had plenty of times where I told myself I ought to do better, you know, like you do with anything. Back when I was working routes, if I got behind or started grabbing food from gas stations, I’d say, “Alright, tomorrow I’m going to be more sensible,” but then tomorrow comes and you’re thinking about deliveries, traffic, where the next stop is, all that.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’ve made a real plan to cut down, if that’s what you mean. I’ve thought about it, sure, but I’m not sure I’d call it a proper attempt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to avoid certain foods or situations because you thought they might trigger one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really avoided things like that, no. Not in any organized way.\n\nI mean, there are foods I’ll think, “Well, maybe don’t bring that home,” but then I’m at the store and I’m thinking about whether the truck needs gas, or whether I forgot something, and next thing I know I’m comparing prices on crackers or whatever. I used to know every good place to stop on certain routes—some stations had better coffee, one had those sandwiches that looked terrible but somehow you’d still get one. Funny what sticks in your head.\n\nBut as far as actually saying, “I can’t go there” or “I have to stay away from that food because it’ll set me off”… I’m not sure I’ve done that. Not clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything to try to make up for the amount of food you ate during one of these episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know what you mean by “make up for it,” exactly.\n\nLike, I’ve told myself, “Well, you shouldn’t have done that,” and maybe the next day I’ll think I ought to be more careful.
